=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a string s and an integer t, representing the number of transformations to perform. In one transformation, every character in s is replaced according to the following rules:

If the character is 'z', replace it with the string "ab".
Otherwise, replace it with the next character in the alphabet. For example, 'a' is replaced with 'b', 'b' is replaced with 'c', and so on.

Return the length of the resulting string after exactly t transformations.
Since the answer may be very large, return it modulo 10^9 + 7.
 
Example 1:

Input: s = "abcyy", t = 2
Output: 7
Explanation:

First Transformation (t = 1):

	
'a' becomes 'b'
'b' becomes 'c'
'c' becomes 'd'
'y' becomes 'z'
'y' becomes 'z'
String after the first transformation: "bcdzz"


Second Transformation (t = 2):
	
'b' becomes 'c'
'c' becomes 'd'
'd' becomes 'e'
'z' becomes "ab"
'z' becomes "ab"
String after the second transformation: "cdeabab"


Final Length of the string: The string is "cdeabab", which has 7 characters.


Example 2:

Input: s = "azbk", t = 1
Output: 5
Explanation:

First Transformation (t = 1):

	
'a' becomes 'b'
'z' becomes "ab"
'b' becomes 'c'
'k' becomes 'l'
String after the first transformation: "babcl"


Final Length of the string: The string is "babcl", which has 5 characters.


 
Constraints:

1 <= s.length <= 10^5
s consists only of lowercase English letters.
1 <= t <= 10^5

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def lengthAfterTransformations(self, s: str, t: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

    def lengthAfterTransformations(self, s: str, t: int) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7

So final should be:
```python
class Solution:
    def lengthAfterTransformations(self, s: str, t: int) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        cnt = [0] * 26
        for ch in s:
            cnt[ord(ch) - ord('a')] += 1

        for _ in range(t):
            nxt = [0] + cnt[:25]
            nxt[0] = cnt[25]
            nxt[1] = (nxt[1] + cnt[25]) % MOD
            cnt = nxt

        return sum(cnt) % MOD
```

</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
class Solution:
    def lengthAfterTransformations(self, s: str, t: int) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        cnt = [0] * 26
        base = ord('a')

        for ch in s:
            cnt[ord(ch) - base] += 1

        for _ in range(t):
            nxt = [0] + cnt[:25]   # a..y shift to b..z
            nxt[0] = cnt[25]       # z -> a
            nxt[1] = (nxt[1] + cnt[25]) % MOD  # z -> b
            cnt = nxt

        return sum(cnt) % MOD
```
